SECTION 4. APPLICABLE REVIEW STANDARDS AND CRITERIA AND FINDINGS OF 
FACT 
4.1 
Section 19 64 060 - General Requirements for New Dwellings 
A. 
The proposed non-farm dwelling will not force a significant change in or 
significantly increase the cost of accepted farm or forest practices on surrounding 
lands devoted to farm or forest use; 
· 
Dirling - Non-Farm Dwelling 
~bit D - 1 
Finding: Based on the surrounding development pattern, there appears to be minimal 
active farm or forest use in the immediate vicinity. The subject parcel is bordered by 
existing rural residential development on three sides, with the Rogue River located to the 
south As such, if the application had been approved, the development does not appear 
to adversely affect or increase the cost of accepted farm or forest practices on surrounding 
resource lands. 
4.2 
B. 
As condition of approval, the landowner for the dwelling shall sign and record in 
the county deed records a document binding the landowner, and the landowner's 
successors in interest, prohibiting them from pursuing claim for relief or cause of 
action alleging injury from farming or forest practices for which no action or claim 
is allowed under ORS 30.936 or 30.937. 
Finding: If application had been approved, the applicant would be required to submit a 
signed, notarized, and recorded Waiver of Remonstrance Regarding Dwellings in Farm 
Use. 
Section 19.64.070.A - Standards and Criteria for Non-Farm Dwellings 
A. 
Dwellings Not in Conjunction with Farm Use (Non-Farm Dwellings). One 
single-family residential dwelling not provided in conjunction with commercial farm 
use, based on findings demonstrating that all of the following requirements are 
met: 
1. 
The dwelling is situated upon a lot or parcel, or a portion of a lot or parcel, which 
is generally unsuitable land for the production of farm crops and livestock or 
merchantable tree species, considering the terrain, adverse soil or land conditions, 
drainage and flooding, vegetation, location and size of the tract. 
a. 
A lot or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel shall not be considered unsuitable 
solely because of size or location if it can reasonably be put to farm or forest 
use in conjunction with other land; and 
b. 
A lot or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel is not "generally unsuitable" 
simply because it is too small to be farmed profitably by itself. If a lot or 
parcel or portion of a lot or parcel can be sold, leased, rented or otherwise 
managed as a part of a commercial farm or ranch, then the lot or parcel or 
portion of the lot or parcel is not "generally unsuitable." A lot or parcel or 
portion of a lot or parcel is presumed to be suitable if it is _composed 
predominantly of Class I-IV soils. Just because a lot or parcel or portion 
of a lot or parcel is unsuitable for one farm use does not mean it is not 
suitable for another farm use; or 
c. 
If the parcel is under forest assessment, the dwelling shall be situated upon 
generally unsuitable land for the production of merchantable tree species 
recognized by the forest practices rules, considering the terrain, adverse 
soil or land conditions, drainage and flooding, vegetation, location and 
size of the parcel. In addition: 
(1] If a lot or parcel is under forest assessment, the area is not "generally 
unsuitable" simply because it is too small to be managed for forest 
Dirling - Non-Farm Dwelling 
~i4:,it D - 2 
production profitably by itself. If a lot or parcel under forest assessment can 
be sold, leased, rented or otherwise managed as a part of a forestry 
operation, it is not "generally unsuitable." 
[2] If a lot or parcel is under forest assessment, it is presumed suitable if it 
is composed predominantly of soils capable of producing 50 cubic feet of 
wood fiber per acre per year; 
d. 
If a lot or parcel is under forest assessment, a finding must be made that 
the dwelling is compatible with and will not seriously interfere with forest 
uses on surrounding lands and will not force a significant change in forest 
practices or significantly increase the cost of those practices on the 
surrounding lands. 
Finding: The subject property is approximately 2. 71 acres in size and contains two 
mapped soil types: Camas gravelly sandy loam (14), comprising approximately 16 percent 
of the site and rated as Class IV if irrigated and non-irrigated, and Newberg fine sandy 
loam (57), comprising approximately 84 percent of the site and rated as Class II if irrigated 
and non-irrigated. Because the parcel is composed predominantly of Class I-IV soils, the 
parcel is presumed to be suitable for farm use pursuant to the applicable code criteria. 
The proposed homesite is located within the portion of the property mapped as Newberg 
fine sandy loam, which is rated as Class fl soil and is generally well suited for irrigated hay 
and pasture production. In addition, the Josephine County soil mapping indicates that the 
majority of the property consists of prime soils if irrigated, and the property has water rights 
through the Fort Vannoy Irrigation District under Certificate 85233 (Exhibit CJ. 
Although the parcel is physically separated from adjacent resource lands by existing 
development features, including a shared asphalt driveway to the west serving multiple 
residences and an irrigation canal to the north, Planning cannot determine that the parcel 
or portion of the parcel cannot reasonably be sold, leased, rented, or otherwise managed 
as part of a commercial farm or ranch operation due to the predominance of Class I-IV 
soils and the agricultural capability classifications of the property. 
According to the Josephine County Assessor's records, the property is not currently under 
forest assessment; therefore, the provisions of Section 19.64.070(A)(1)(c) and (d), JCC, 
do not apply. 
2. 
The dwelling will not materially alter the stability of the overall land use pattern on 
the area subject to OAR 660-033-0130(4)(c)(C). 
Finding: The applicant submitted a cumulative impacts and land use pattern analysis to 
evaluate whether the proposed nonfarm dwelling would materially alter the stability of the 
surrounding land use pattern, as required by OAR 660-033-0130(4){c)(C). The study area 
encompasses approximately 2,036.91 acres of adjacent and nearby properties and 
includes 183 Exclusive Farm (EF) zoned tax lots. Within ine study area, 126 tax lots 
contain existing dwellings and 57 tax lots do not contain dwellings. Of the 126 developed 
lots, 87 were identified as containing farm dwellings and 39 were identified as containing 
nonfarm dwellings. Farm versus nonfarm dwelling status was evaluated using available 
Josephine County Assessor records and corroborated with aerial and historical imagery 
where necessary. The analysis demonstrates that approximately 68 percent of tne parcels 
Dirling - Non-Farm Dwelling 
within the study area contain dwellings, with an average parcel size of approximately 11. 13 
acres. This reflects an established pattern of rural residential development within the EF 
zone rather than large contiguous blocks devoted exclusively to commercial agricultural 
use. The applicant also evaluated the 57 undeveloped parcels within the study area to 
determine their potential eligibility for nonfarm dwellings. The evaluation considered 
predominant soil classifications, whether the parcels could reasonably be managed in 
conjunction with adjacent farm operations, the character of surrounding land uses, and 
the existing development pattern in the vicinity. Based on those factors, the analysis 
concluded that approximately six parcels within the study area may potentially qualify for 
nonfarm dwellings. Based on the evidence submitted, 
if the application had been 
approved, the proposed dwelling does not appear likely to materially alter the stability of 
the overall land use pattern within the surrounding area subject to OAR 660-033- 
0130( 4)(c)(C). 
4.3 
3. 
The dwelling will be sited on a lot or parcel created before January 1, 1993, or on 
a lot or parcel created after January 1, 1993, when the lot or parcel was created in 
conformance with the requirements of Section JCC 19.64.090.C. 
Finding: Planning and Assessor records indicate that the subject parcel (Tax Lot 2800) 
was originally created by Warranty Deed (V327 P786) on October 27, 1977. The parcel 
was subsequently reconfigured through Partition Plat 2025-31 on September 16, 2025, 
resulting in its current size of 2. 71 acres. Because the parcel was originally created prior 
to January 1, 1993, and the subsequent reconfiguration was completed in conformance 
with applicable County land division requirements, If the application had been approved 
the parcel satisfies the requirements of this section. 
4. 
A non-farm dwelling may not be approved on a lot or parcel that is already 
established with a single-family dwelling is established on a lot or parcel as a lot of 
record dwelling per subsection 19.64.070.B, JCC. 
Finding: Planning and Assessor records indicate that the parcel is currently vacant, and 
no other dwelling exists on the property. 
5. 
Applicant shall submit proof that the lot or parcel has been disqualified for valuation 
for farm use under ORS 308A 050 to 308A.128 or other special assessment under 
ORS 308A315, 321.257 to 321.390, 321700 to 321.754 or 321.805 to 321.855. 
and any additional tax imposed as the result of disqualification has been paid 
pursuant to the requirement of ORS 215.236. 
Finding: Per the Josephine County Assessor records the subject property is not receiving 
special farm assessment. 
Section 19.64.080 - Siting Standards 
A. 
The dwelling or activities associated with the dwelling will not force a significant 
change in or significantly increase the cost of accepted farming or forest practices 
on nearby lands devoted to farm or forest use; 
Finding: If application had been approved it does not appear that the proposed 
development would materially interfere with accepted farm or forest practices in the area 
Dirling - Non-Farm Dwelling 
4.4 
B. 
The placement of dwellings shall be located on the least productive, buildable 
portion of the parcel taking into consideration terrain, adverse soil or land 
conditions, drainage and flooding, access, vegetation, location and the size of the 
tract; 
Finding: The applicant has submitted a no-rise certification prepared by a registered 
engineer in accordance with Chapter 19. 69A, JCC. Any change in homesite location 
requires a new study. 
C. 
A lot or parcel shall not be considered unsuitable solely because of size or location 
if it can be reasonably be put to farm or forest use in conjunction with other land. 
Finding: The subject parcel is not considered unsuitable solely because of its size or 
location. Based on the mapped soil classifications, including the presence of Class II ancl 
other agriculturally capable soils, the parcel appears to be capable of being reasonably 
put to farm or forest use in conjunction with other land. 
D. 
If the parcel is under forest assessment, the dwelling shall be sited upon generally 
unsuitable land for the production of merchantable tree species recognized under 
the Forest Practice Rules considering terrain, adverse soil or land conditions, 
drainage and flooding, vegetation, location and size of the parcel; 
Finding: Per the Josephine County Assessor records the subject property is not receiving 
special forest assessment. 
E. 
The dwelling will not materially alter the stability of the overall land use pattern of 
the area; 
Finding: As discussed under Section 4.2.A(2) above, the dwelling does not appear to 
materially alter the stability of the overall land use pattern in the area. 
F. 
If the dwelling is established under JCC 19.64.070.A or B, then additional dwellings 
may not be approved or sited. 
Finding: No additional dwellings were proposed. 
Section 19.69A.060 -Administration in floodway areas 
The property contains the floodplain of the Rogue River. The proposed home-site is 
entirely within the floodway. The structure shall be located at the site which is least 
impacted by inundation. The dwelling shall meet all development permit requirements of 
Section 19.69A.080 JCC and all property development standards of Section 19.69A.090 
JCC. 
Finding: The applicant submitted a No-Rise Flood Analysis prepared by the Galli Group, 
which appears to demonstrate that the proposed development will not result in an increase 
in base flood elevation within the floodway. However, if the application had been approved, 
the applicant would have been required to submit an application for a Technical Plan 
Review to the Planning Department for floodplain review and verification of compliance 
with Sections 19. 69A. 080 and 19. 69A. 090, JCC. Any change in homesite location requires 
a new study. 
Dirling - Non-Farm Dwelling 
4.5 
Section 19.76.020.D-Wildfire and Emergency Safety Standards 
New dwellings in any zone other than the forest zone shall demonstrate compliance with 
all of the applicable standards of Chapter 19. 76 JCC. 
Finding: If the application had been approved, the applicant would have been required to 
submit a wildfire safety plan demonstrating compliance with the requirements of Chapter 
19.76, JCC. 
4.6 
Section 19.83.020 - Application of Standards (Erosion Confrol) 
These standards apply to any land use application including development and 
construction which would require any grading or filling on slopes that are 15% or greater 
or soils that are granitic in composition as mapped by the NRCS except when authorized 
or regulated by the Oregon Forest Practices Act. 
Finding: The property contains granitic soils. If the application had been approved, the 
applicant would have been required to submit an erosion and sediment control plan. 
4. 7 
Public Comments: 
Staff received one comment within the comment period from Steve Rouse, Rogue 
Advocates, PO Box 624, Ashland, Oregon 97520. The comment stated that under 
Section 19.64.070A 1, JCC, the applicant must demonstrate that the property is 
generally unsuitable for the production of farm crops, livestock, or merchantable 
tree species. The comment referenced NRCS soil classifications and noted that 
lands comprised predominantly of Class I-IV soils are generally presumed suitable 
for farm use. The comment further stated that land is not considered generally 
unsuitable solely because it is too small to farm independently if it can reasonably 
be managed in conjunction with adjacent commercial farm operations. The 
comment also referenced the productive alluvial soils commonly associated with 
the Rogue River corridor and requested findings addressing compliance with 
Section 19.64.070A2, JCC, regarding impacts to the overall land use pattern, 
including consideration of floodplain, riparian, scenic, and surrounding farm and 
forest practices. In addition, the comment requested confirmation that the 
proposed dwelling is located on the least productive portion of the parcel and will 
not significantly interfere with accepted farm or forest practices. 
Staff also received one written comment outside the applicable comment period 
from Buck and Linda Baker, 4707 Lower River Road, Grants Pass, Oregon 97526. 
The comment stated that the they have resided on the adjacent property since 
1996 and have direct knowledge of the development history of the subject parcel 
has produced hay crops in the past and is bound by the Fort Vannoy Irrigation 
District services ditch for water purposes. The comment referenced Oregon 
Statewide Planning Goal 3 and expressed concerns regarding the protection of 
agricultural land, open space, scenic resources, and wildlife habitat from 
residential development. While there are some residential homes on large pieces 
of acreage, the homes typically are along the Rogue River or on Lower River Rd, 
not in the middle of a hayfield like subject property. There are multiple commercial 
agricultural operations that operate on even smaller parcels within the immediate 
area of subject parcel The neighboring lot owned by the same owner has a home, 
Detached Living Space, and large recreational vehicle building. 
Dirling - Non-Farm Dwelling 
The applicant submitted a written response addressing concerns related to soil 
suitability and the "generally unsuitable" standard, cumulative impacts on the land 
use pattern, the sensitivity of the Rogue River corridor, farm use disqualification, 
siting of the dwelling on the least productive portion of the parcel, and potential 
impacts on surrounding farm and forest practices referring to the narrative 
submitted as part of the application addressing these concerns. 
Finding: Staff reviewed the submitted materials and addressed the identified 
issues in the findings above. 
SECTION 5. DECISION 
Based on the evidence and the findings of fact entered herein, the Planning Director finds that 
the application has not met the burden of proof. A proposed dwelling is not allowed if the tract 
upon which it would be sited already includes a dwelling, and the two other parcels in the tract 
contain residences. Therefore, the Planning Director hereby issues a decision of DENIAL for a 
forest dwelling on the subject property. 
Done and dated this 22nd day of May, 2026.

Note: According to Oregon law your dwelling approval is valid for a period of 4 years starting from 
the date of approval shown above. You are entitled to obtain a one-time 2-year extension of the 
dwelling approval as long as a written request is received by 6 year expiration date. While the 
permit is valid you must initiate substantial residential development (see definition below). By 
obtaining a development permit for the dwelling and complete construction before the original or 
extended time limit expires, the development is considered complete. 
If you are not able to do 
this, then you must request a determination of substantial development. To do this, you will need 
to file a Determination of Development application, together with a review fee.

physical site, structure and/or facility development:

Exhibit F - 3 
Irrigated Capability Class-Josephine County, Oregon 
Description 
Land capability classification shows, in a general way, the suitability of soils for 
most kinds of field crops. Crops that require special management are excluded. 
The soils are grouped according to their limitations for field crops, the risk of 
damage if they are used for crops, and the way they respond to management. 
The criteria used in grouping the soils do not include major and generally 
expensive landforming that would change slope, depth, or other characteristics of 
the soils, nor do they include possible but unlikely major reclamation projects. 
Capability classification is not a substitute for interpretations that show suitability 
and limitations of groups of soils for rangeland, for woodland, or for engineering 
purposes. 
In the capability system, soils are generally grouped at three levels-capability 
class, subclass, and unit. Only class and subclass are included in this data set. 
Capability classes, the broadest groups, are designated by the numbers 1 
through 8. The numbers indicate progressively greater limitations and narrower 
choices for practical use. The classes are defined as follows: 
Class 1 soils have few limitations that restrict their use. 
Class 2 soils have moderate limitations that reduce the choice of plants or that 
require moderate conservation practices. 
Class 3 soils have severe limitations that reduce the choice of plants or that 
require special conservation practices, or both. 
Class 4 soils have very severe limitations that reduce the choice of plants or that 
require very careful management, or both. 
Class 5 soils are subject to little or no erosion but have other limitations, 
impractical to remove, that restrict their use mainly to pasture, rangeland, 
forestland, or wildlife habitat. 
Class 6 soils have severe limitations that make them generally unsuitable for 
cultivation and that restrict their use mainly to pasture, rangeland, forestland, or 
wildlife habitat. 
Class 7 soils have very severe limitations that make them unsuitable for 
cultivation and that restrict their use mainly to grazing, forestland, or wildlife 
habitat. 
Class 8 soils and miscellaneous areas have limitations that preclude commercial 
plant production and that restrict their use to recreational purposes, wildlife 
habitat, watershed, or esthetic purposes. 
Rating Options 
Aggregation Method: Dominant Condition 
Component Percent Cutoff: None Specified

EXHIBIT "J" 
COMMENTS FROM NEIGHBORS 
OPPOSITION 
Attached is a letter, for your consideration. Lindi Baker and I submitted it to the 
Planning Department when the Department was considering the Applicant's 
variance application which, of course, was denied. In addition to our original 
letter, following are several additional objections we have to the issuance of a 
variance for the subject property. 
The Exclusive Fann zoning carries with it some great disadvantages and some 
advantages for property owners and the community in general. One disadvantage 
as a property owner is that we now have to live with all the marijuana farms and all 
of the transients which they employ. One important advantage is that we should 
not have to worry about a subdivision going in next door. That is exactly what is 
happening at the Applicant's property next door. We believe they have abused the 
system in several major ways. 
First, we believe the Applicants did more than a 50% remodel on their primary 
river home with no notification to FEMA or adherence to flood requirements. 
Next, the Applicants built an "Additional Living Space" house (Not an Additional 
Dwelling Unit) that is a large structure and includes a full kitchen and many 
amenities. This structure was then advertised on the internet as an "Air B & B" or 
"VRBO" which we believe is illegal in EF zoning (see attached pictures). They 
also have promoted their property as a River Venue for profit which also is 
improper and illegal in the EF zoning. Josephine County Planning called them in 
to tell them that these activities are not permitted in EF zoning but it appears they 
continue to rent the house and have venue activities. Josephine County Planning 
tells us that there is no enforcement division in our county, so on it goes. 
They also built a huge RV barn, crosswise to the flow of the river, which, we 
believe, does not have proper flowthrough capabilities, and we are convinced, 
based on our experience with flooding on these properties, that this building will 
increase the rise in the river at our house during the next flood. We have 
experienced two floods and are very aware of the river flow in the specific area of 
the RV barn. 
The Applicants also own a rental house directly in front of their property, on Lower 
River Road. We are concerned, based on their past activities, that they will seek to 
build yet another "Additional Living Space" on that property. That would make 
five residential homes on a conjoined EF property that needs a special 
accommodation for only two. The entire acreage of the two original lots ( original 
river house and original rental house on Lower River Road) is far less than what is 
required for one residential home in ER zoning absent special accommodations 
with a variance. This fact alone broadcasts how contrary the current and requested 
Exhibit J - 1 
development of the properties are to the stated purposes of the EF zoning 
regulations and objectives. This is zoned to be exclusive farm land, not residential 
subdivisions. 
They have already drilled a well on the subject parcel in anticipation of obtaining 
first the variance and now prevailing on this appeal. Our well on our property has 
dropped in production over the last thirty years, probably due to the massive 
amount of water being used by the now surrounding marijuana farms. Another 
well and residence will undoubtedly delete the water table even more. 
Lastly, the original application was denied because the Applicants failed to 
demonstrate that the soils on the subject property were non-resource (not 
agriculture soils). Our property, right next door to the east, touching their property, 
has the most beautiful black river loam imaginable. Indeed, all of these properties 
were historically a part of the Rogue River basin. Likewise, the adjacent property 
to the west of the subject property abounds with agriculture production over the 
years of hay, orchards and gardens, both private and commercial. There is no way 
that they can support the notion that their property is non-resource (not agricultural 
soils). 
All of the planning and zoning rules are there for a purpose. We are in Exclusive 
Farm zoning and such misuse should not be allowed. 
Exhibit J - 2 
This is an "Additional Living Space". It is 
not even legal to build an Additional 
Dwelling Unit (ADU) in EF zoning. 
Exhibit J - 3

Soil Quality 
4 707 Lower River Rd and Lot expanded by Lot Line Adjustment 
contingent to 4707 Lower River Rd and 4735 Lower River Rd 
(Subject Lot-Map#3 606 l 5C0002800 TL#002800) 
The subject lot (TL#2800) is surrounded by many agricultural lots that 
are in high agricultural production. The famous Krause Farms is just 
down the road. Several "Herb" farms with high production are very 
close as is an orchard and high production hay field. 4 707 Lower River 
Road is right next door and it has dark brown, rich, river loam (see 
picture) which supports a high production garden. 
In 1964 the Rogue River flooded and eroded a shallow trench across 
both 4707 Lower River Road and 4735 Lower River Road (the address 
of the subject lot at that time). The Army Corp of Engineers came in 
and filled the trench with some inferior, rocky soil. This area is only a 
very small fraction of the land area of subject lot. If applicants should 
submit this soil as representing the soil on their lot, it should be 
disregarded as not being a true representative of soil making up subject 
lot. 
All of the planning and zoning rules are there for a purpose. We are in 
Exclusive Farm zoning and such misuse, as building another residence, 
should not be allowed.

Luis Hernandez support quotes 
To whom it may concern, 
On the morning of July 3, 2026 at 10 AM we, Raymond Dirling and Amanda Dirling had a conversation with 
Luis S Hernandez, The homeowner of 4803 Lower River Road Grants Pass, OR 97526. Louis Hernandez 
has been farming his 20 acres for the past couple of decades. He is an alfalfa hay and hazelnut farmer. 
He currently has 281 hazelnut trees on his property which is adjacent to the subject properties shared 
driveway. 
He received a letter from the county stating that our approval to build our one-story cottage had been 
denied. He told us because he is 87, He feels he would not like to write a letter however, if his late wife 
was still here, "she would have written one already." He gave us permission to "please quote him" by 
stating that in his opinion, the EFU land in subject that we have applied to build a house on is not 
farmable land and he would "never farm it". He said "it is too small". He said that "The county should let 
us build our cottage for our elderly parents to age in place in, and he would be happy to see us build." He 
also said that "as a neighbor to the property in question he has no reason to not build but wish's that the 
county would vote to let us build." 
Thank you so much for your time and your consideration, 
Amanda and Raymond Dirling

EXHIBIT "L" 
COMMENTS FROM NEIGHBORS SUPPORT 
Emily Bertman 
4751 Lower River Rd 
Grants Pass, Oregon 
June 16, 2025 
Josephine County Planning Department 
Grants Pass, Oregon 
Re: Letter of Support - 
Dirling Family Non-Farm Dwelling Application 
To the Josephine County Planning Department: 
My name is Emily Bertman, and I am a neighboring property owner at 4751 Lower River 
Rd in Grants Pass. I am writing in strong support of the Dirling family's application to 
construct a non-farm dwelling on their property, and to respectfully encourage the 
Department to reconsider its denial of that request. 
I have come to know the Dirling family through our shared road and have spoken at 
length with their son, Ray Jr., about their situation. His parents are aging and are no 
longer able to comfortably navigate the stairs in their current home. The proposed 
dwelling would allow them to remain close to their family, on property their family 
already owns, in a home designed to meet their needs as they get older. Meanwhile, 
Ray Jr. would move from the existing accessory dwelling unit into the parents' current 
home, making full use of the existing structures. This is a thoughtful, family-centered 
plan that causes no waste of resources and no disruption to the surrounding area. 
I understand that the denial was based on a determination that the Dirlings' land is 
farmable. As a neighboring landowner and an active organic farmer myself, I want to 
offer my perspective on that characterization. The field in question lies directly across 
the driveway from my property, and it is my observation, shared, I understand, by at 
least three other farmers who have assessed the land, that it cannot support even basic 
forage crops such as hay. The proposed home site is located near the existing 
residence, and would have minimal footprint relative to the parcel as a whole. In my 
view, designating this ground as productive farmland when experienced local farmers 
have found it unproductive would be a hardship on the Dirling family without a 
corresponding agricultural benefit to the community. 
I hope the Department will weigh this appeal with the full picture in mind- a family 
working to care for their elderly parents with dignity, on land that holds little practical 
agricultural value, with a plan that is sensible, modest in scope, and deeply rooted in the 
kind of rural community values we all share here in the Rogue Valley. I wholeheartedly 
support the Dirling family's request and ask that it be approved. 
With appreciation for your consideration, 
Emily Bertman

COMMENTS FROM NEIGHBORS SUPPORT 
Justin Gerlitz

Gabrielle Fey 
4 711 Lower River Rd.

Josephine County Planning Department 
Grants Pass, Oregon 
Re: Letter of Support for the Durling Family Non-Farm Dwelling Application 
To the Josephine County Planning Department, 
I am writing in support of the Durling family's application to construct a single-family 
residence on their property. 
I currently live at 4 711 Lower River Road, a rental home owned by the Durling family, 
and I also own the neighboring riverfront property immediately adjacent to theirs. Having 
lived next to the family and observed their commitment to one another, I have come to 
appreciate that this request is not about development or financial gain, but about caring 
for aging parents while keeping multiple generations together. 
Ray and his wife are now in their eighties and currently live in a beautiful riverfront 
home that, because of floodplain regulations, requires climbing stairs to access the living 
areas. As people age, stairs become increasingly difficult and significantly increase the 
risk of falls and serious injury. The family's proposal to build a modest, single-story 
home would allow them to remain safely on the property they have long called home 
while continuing to live close to their children and grandchildren. 
As a Family Nurse Practitioner, I have seen firsthand the importance of allowing older 
adults to remain near family whenever possible. Keeping aging parents close to those 
who can assist with daily needs often helps preserve independence, improves quality of 
life, and can delay or prevent the need for more intensive care. Throughout my career, I 
have supported requests for reasonable accommodations that enable families to care for 
elderly loved ones at home because these arrangements often provide the safest and most 
compassionate solution. 
1 
Exhibit M - 1 
The Durling family's proposal reflects those same values. Their son and daughter-in-law 
would move into the existing home, allowing them to assist with maintenance of the 
property and provide support as Ray and his wife continue to age. This plan strengthens 
an existing family unit while allowing the older generation to remain in familiar 
surroundings with dignify. 
I understand that the County must balance the preservation of Exclusive Farm Use land 
with the needs of property owners. In this case, I respectfully ask that you also consider 
the human aspect of this request. This is a long-established family seeking a reasonable 
accommodation that allows them to care for elderly parents without altering the overall 
character of the property or the surrounding neighborhood. 
For these reasons, I respectfully encourage the Planning Department to approve the 
Durling family's application. 
Thank you for your time and thoughtful consideration. 
Sincerely, 
Gabrielle Fey

"EXHIBIT N" RECOMMENDED CONDITIONS OF APPROVAL - Non-Farm Dwelling 
Based on the evidence and the findings of fact entered herein, the BCC hereby APPROVES the 
request for the Non-Farm Dwelling as described herein. This approval is subject to the 
performance of the following conditions: 
1. The dwelling, driveway, and fire safety measures must be sited on the property within the 
building envelope as depicted on the attached plot plan map marked as Exhibit A. (JCC 
Section 19.64.080) Dwelling and structures must be able to maintain the required 100' 
secondary fire safety zone within the building envelope. If the dwelling is to be relocated 
outside an approved building envelope, an application for a Homesite Re-site is required. 
2. Applicant shall submit an application for a Tech Plan Review ($412) to the Planning 
Department for floodplain review and verification of compliance with Sections 19.69A.080 
and Section 19.69A.090. JCC. Any changes to the homesite location require a new study. 
3. The applicant shall submit an application. an access permit from Public Works, and pay 
the current fee of $155.00 for a new address. 
4. A Development Permit ($392) must be issued as the final authorization for this approval. 
(JCC Section 19.22.040.C.4) 
Prior to the issuance of a Development Permit. the applicant must: 
a. Submit an application for the dwelling, floor plan & elevations, a revised tentative plan 
map, to include location of well, septic, proof of water, and fire safety zones. (Section 
19.76.080.C, JCC) 
b. Submit a copy of the recorded Waiver of Remonstrance for Farm Uses to the Planning 
Office. (Section 19.64.060, JCC) 
c. Submit valid proof of fire protection from a contracted fire protection servicer. (JCC 
Section 19.76.060) 
5. Once you obtain an approved Development Permit, the applicant shall submit the permit 
to Building Safety and the DEQ (septic) and acquire the appropriate permits and 
inspections. You must comply with all standards required by the Building Safety and DEQ. 
6. Prior to the issuance of a Certificate of Occupancy from the Josephine County Building 
Safety Department, the owner shall demonstrate the wildfire safety plan approved by this 
decision has been fully implemented on the property. Documents shall include: 
a. 
A completed Wildfire and Emergency Safety Standards Checklist for Forest Zones. 
b. 
A letter from your fire service provider approving the driveway.

Important Notes: According to Oregon law your dwelling approval is valid for a period of 4 years 
starting from the date of approval shown above. You are entitled to obtain a one-time 2-year 
extension of the dwelling approval as long as a written request for extension is received within 6 
years of the initial approval date. While the permit is valid you must initiate substantial residential 
development (see definition below). The safest way to do this is by obtaining a development 
permit for the dwelling and complete construction before the original or extended time limit 
expires. 
If you are not able to do this, then you must request a determination of substantial development. 
To do this, you will need to file a Determination of Development application, together with a review 
fee, and meet this standard: 
SUBSTANTIAL DEVELOPMENT: Actions taken by the permit holder which 
encompass more than simple site preparation and grading. When actual site 
and/or physical improvements that constitute a considerable part of the whole 
project have been lawfully undertaken, such improvements must encompass more 
than simple site preparation or surveying, and must include physical site, structure 
and/or facility development: 
A. 
For individual home site approvals, substantial development means: 
1. 
A development permit as required by the land use approval 
has been issued; and 
2. 
Evidence the following site improvements have been 
installed: 
a. 
A septic system, or approved connection to a 
sewerage system provided for the property; or,

A system of delivering an approved source of water 
supply to the property (i.e., well report and 
registration

Resources Department); verified for domestic use 
by a water quality report that confirms standards for 
potable water are met.

Statement of Appeal: Appellant objects to the Director's decision to deny the placement 
of Non-Farm Dwelling located on the subject property. See 
Appellant's appeal application attached as (Exhibit A).

36-06-15-C0 TL 2800

APPEAL BACKGROUND 
• The application was submitted on February 6, 2026. 
• Application was deemed complete on March 5, 2026. 
• Notice of Land Use Request mailed on March 17, 2026. 
APPEAL BACKGROUND (cont.) 
• The Director issued a decision on May 22, 2026, denying the 
proposed Non-Farm Dwelling on the basis that the subject parcel 
is composed predominantly of Class II soils and mapped as prime 
soils if irrigated therefore cannot be considered generally 
unsuitable for farm use (Exhibit D). The Notice of Decision was 
mailed out on June 15, 2026 (Exhibit E). 
REVIEW 
• The Board of County Commissioners (hereafter BCC) must 
determine if the application complies with the criteria listed in 
order to determine a Non-Farm Dwelling approval. 
• Given this hearing is de novo (new), the Board now has the task 
to decide if the application meets the standards and criteria. 
The Board may agree, disagree, or alter the findings as needed 
after receiving testimony during the hearing. The Board may 
change, add, and delete any conditions listed in the decision if 
the application is approved. If the application is denied, the 
Board must make clear findings as such. 
REVIEW (cont.) 
During the review of the application, staff evaluated the proposal 
for the placement of a non-farm dwelling under the applicable 
Standards and Criteria. Based on that review, the application was 
denied. The denial was issued because the majority of the subject 
property consists of predominantly Class II soils. These soils are 
identified by the National Resources Conservation Service 
(NRCS) as prime farmland soils (Exhibit F) and are also classified 
as prime farmland if irrigated under Josephine County soil 
mapping (Exhibit G). 
In addition, the property has existing 
irrigation water rights as documented by the Oregon Water 
Resources Department (OWRD) (Exhibit H).

ORDINANCE REVIEW 
This application is subject to Section 19.64.060 - General Criteria for 
New Dwellings; Section 19.64.070.A - Standards and Criteria for 
Non-Farm Dwellings; Section 19.64.080 - Siting Standards; Section 
19.69A.060 - Administration in floodway areas; Chapter 19. 76 - 
Wildfire & Emergency Safety Standards; Section 19.83.020 - Erosion 
Control. 
Section 19.64.070.A - Standards and 
Criteria for Non-Farm Dwellings 
A. Dwellings Not in Conjunction with Farm Use (Non-Farm Dwellings) 
a. A lot or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel shall not be considered unsuitable 
solely because of size or location if it can reasonably be put to farm or forest use 
in conjunction with other land; and 
b. A lot or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel is not "generally unsuitable" simply 
because it is too small to be farmed profitably by itself. 
If a lot or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel can be sold, leased, rented or otherwise 
managed as a part of a commercial farm or ranch, then the lot or parcel or portion of the lot 
or parcel is not "generally unsuitable." 
A lot or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel is presumed to be suitable if it is composed 
predominantly of Class I-IV soils. 
Section 19.64.070.A - Standards and 
Criteria for Non-Farm Dwellings 
The subject property is approximately 2.71 acres in size and contains two mapped soil types: 
► Camas gravelly sandy loam (14), comprising approximately 16 percent of the site and rated Class 
IV. 
► Newberg fine sandy loam (57), comprising approximately 84 percent of the site and rated Class II. 
• The parcel is composed predominantly of Class I-IV soils, the parcel is presumed to be suitable for farm 
use pursuant to the applicable code criteria. 
• 
The proposed homesite is located within the portion of the property mapped as Newberg fine sandy 
loam, which is rated as Class II soil and is generally well suited for irrigated hay and pasture production. 
• The Josephine County soil mapping indicates that the majority of the property consists of prime soils if 
irrigated, and the property has water rights through the Fort Vannoy Irrigation District under Certificate 
85233. 
Section 19.64.070.A - Standards and 
Criteria for Non-Farm Dwellings 
Although the parcel is physically separated from adjacent resource 
lands by existing development features, including a shared asphalt 
driveway to the west serving multiple residences and an irrigation 
, canal to the north, Planning could not determine that the parcel or 
portion of the parcel cannot reasonably be sold, leased, rented, or 
otherwise managed as part of a commercial farm or ranch operation 
due to the predominance of Class I-IV soils and the agricultural 
capability classifications of the property. 
REVIEW (cont.) 
• The Board shall review the appeal application, additional material 
as well as the original decision (Exhibit B) and determine if the 
Criteria listed has been satisfied or can be adequately satisfied 
through conditions of approval. 
• If the Board approves the Non-Farm Dwelling, staff provides 
conditions of approval for consideration in (Exhibit N).

1 
Technical Opinion Regarding Agricultural 
Non-Viability of The Dirling Revocable 
Living Trust's 2. 71 Acre Parcel of 
Undeveloped Land on Lower River Road 
(36-06-15-CO TL 2800) 
This letter is provided to the Dirling Revocable Living Trust to document the expert opinion 
of Richard Vogl, Hydrogeologist, who holds a Bachelors and Master's degree in Geology 
from California State University, Los Angeles, and is a registered geologist in California, 
Certified Hydrogeclogist, and Certified Engineering Geologist, with over 40 years of 
professional experience in the field. This expert opinion is related to the above referenced 
property in Joesphine County, Oregon (Subject Property). 
1. Executive Summary 
The subject property is an undeveloped parcel in southern Oregon with a thin veneer of 
topsoil, ranging from approximately 12 to 36 inches in depth, with an average of 22 inches, 
overlying a dense stream-gravel deposit. These soil conditions were recently assessed and 
documented by numerous potholes conducted across the property by an excavation 
contractor (See attached test pit photos). These physical soil conditions materially restrict 
the effective rooting zone, reduce available water storage, limit nutrient retention, increase 
drought stress, and create severe limitations for sustained crop production. Based on the 
described soil profile, the land should not be characterized as viable cropland without 
extraordinary and economically disproportionate intervention. 
2. Physical Soil Limitations 
Effective soil depth is a controlling factor in crop productivity because plant roots require 
sufficient soil volume for anchorage, water uptake, and nutrient acquisition. Oregon State 
University's forage and soil guidance defines shallow soils as those with 10 to 20 inches 
before a restrictive layer and moderately deep soils as those with 20 to 36 inches before 
such a barrier. A restrictive layer may include gravel, rock, dense clay, or other materials 
that impede root development. The reported 12-to-36-inch topsoil depth across the 
August 5, 2026 
Page 114 
subject property therefore places much of the property within the shallow to moderately 
deep range, with the underlying dense stream-gravel deposit functioning as a practical 
barrier to deeper root development. 
Dense stream gravel is not equivalent to productive agricultural soil. Compared with loam 
or other developed topsoil, coarse gravel has low water-holding capacity, limited fine 
mineral and organic matter content, and reduced ability to retain applied nutrients. During 
dry periods, crops rooted in a thin soil mantle above gravel are more likely to experience 
moisture stress even when regional rainfall is adequate. During wet periods, water may 
move rapidly through coarse material, carrying soluble nutrients below the shallow rooting 
zone and reducing fertilizer efficiency. 
3. Land Capability and Crop Suitability 
USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service land-capability guidance recognizes that 
soil classes reflect limitations for field crops, including inherent soil-property limitations 
such as shallowness, stones, low available water capacity, and low fertility that is difficult 
to correct. Under that framework, soils with severe or very severe limitations may require 
special conservation practices, very careful management, or may be generally unsuitable 
for cultivation. The described property has precisely the type of rooting-zone limitation that 
would reduce crop choice, increase management difficulty, and lower expected yields. 
Even if certain hardy forage, pasture, or dryland species could survive on portions of the 
site, survival is not the same as commercially viable crop production. The relevant 
question is whether the property can reasonably support sustained, ordinary farming for 
marketable crops at yields sufficient to justify the costs. Given the thin topsoil and gravel 
substrate, the expected outcome is reduced crop vigor, uneven stands, vulnerability to 
seasonal drought, inefficient fertilizer use, and materially depressed yield potential. 
4. Economic Non-Viability 
Farming this property would require substantial corrective measures to compensate for the 
shallow soil profile, including imported topsoil or soil amendments, irrigation 
infrastructure, drainage or grading work, repeated nutrient applications, and intensive 
management to address moisture and fertility losses. These measures would impose high 
capital and operating costs without resolving the fundamental limitation: the shallow 
rooting zone over dense gravel. Where land has inherently low yield potential, increased 
inputs often produce diminishing returns rather than profitability. 
August 5, 2026 
Page 214 
Accordingly, the likely agricultural return from the property would be insufficient to support 
a finding that crop farming is an economically reasonable use. A hypothetical ability to 
grow limited vegetation or low-yield crops does not establish economic viability. A viable 
agricultural operation must produce reliable yields at a scale and margin that exceed the 
costs of land preparation, water, fertilizer, equipment, labor, taxes, maintenance, and 
market risk. On this soil profile, those costs would likely exceed the value of any crop 
production. In addition, with the existing shared access easements on the west side of the 
Subject Property, the acreage available for farming is reduced to only 1.27 acres of the total 
acreage, effectively making the Subject Property too small to farm economically even 
without the other farming challenges associated with this property discussed herein. 
5. Greenhouse-Gas and Environmental 
Considerations 
Agricultural activity can contribute to greenhouse-gas emissions through several 
mechanisms, including the use of synthetic and organic fertilizers, irrigation, fuel 
combustion from field equipment, soil disturbance, and related crop-management 
practices. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency identifies agricultural soil 
management as a significant source of nitrous oxide emissions, particularly where nitrogen 
availability is increased through fertilizer and other practices. The Natural Resources 
Conservation Service also recognizes carbon dioxide, methane, and nitrous oxide as 
principal agricultural greenhouse gases and identifies excess nitrogen, fertilizer use, 
reduced energy use, soil disturbance, and cover crops as key climate-related 
considerations. 
Because the property's shallow topsoil and gravel substrate would likely require unusually 
high inputs to obtain even low yields, farming would create a disproportionate 
environmental burden relative to its agricultural benefit. Inputs such as irrigation pumping, 
repeated fertilizer applications, soil amendments, tillage, and machinery use would 
generate greenhouse-gas emissions while delivering limited crop production. In that 
context, converting or forcing the property into crop production would be environmentally 
inefficient and contrary to prudent land-use planning. 
August 5, 2026 
Page314 
6. Conclusion 
For the foregoing reasons, the property should be found unsuitable and not economically 
viable for crop farming. The thin topsoil layer, ranging from approximately 12 to 36 inches, is 
underlain by dense stream gravel that materially restricts root development, water storage, 
nutrient retention, and reliable crop yield. Any agricultural use would likely require 
substantial artificial inputs and management costs that are not justified by the expected 
low productivity. In addition, the greenhouse-gas emissions and environmental impacts 
associated with attempting to farm such marginal land would be disproportionate to any 
limited agricultural output. The more technically sound conclusion is that crop farming is 
not a practical, economically viable, or environmentally responsible use of this property. 
Sincerely, 
Richard A. Vogl, MS, PG, CHG, CEG 
Hydrogeologist 
10909 State Line Road 
Merrill, Oregon 97633

To the County Commissioners Office and Whom it May Concern, 
My name is Caleb Hunter. I am a 4th generation farmer here in 
Southern Oregon. My family owns and operates APPLEGATE 
INVESTMENTS Co. Located at 6955 HWY 238 Jacksonville, OR 97530. We 
mainly farm alfalfa fields and cattle. We have 240 acres under 
cultivation. My family has been farming our land since the 19S0's. I was 
approached by the Dirling family to give my professional opinion on the 
subject property located at ( 36-06-15-CO TL 2800) which is currently 
undeveloped land on Lower River Rd. When researching the property 
location, size and soil I have come to the conclusion that I would NOT 
farm this land. Even if I lived next door. The property size is too small. 
There would be no profit in order to justify the labor and time spent to 
plant any kind of crop. The driveway that the property has an easement 
to use does not allow for my heavy farming equipment to be used 
without harming the road/ driveway that too many other property 
owners use on a daily and also have an easement for use. Lastly, the 
poor soil quality that has been established at this property would not 
satisfy our farming requirements and standards for a favorable and 
successful farm. It is in my professional opinion that the properties best 
use is for generational living. Allowing a small home to be built in order 
for the Dirling's to age gracefully in place and in doing so only using 2% 
of the observed "good soll" is using the property to it's fullest potential 
in both enhancing and preserving value to the property and also 
surrounding· properties. 
Thank you for your consideration and your time, 
Caleb Hunter

A lot or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel is not "generally 
unsuitable" simply because it is too small to be farmed 
profitably by itself. If a lot or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel 
can be sold, leased, rented or otherwise managed as a part of a 
commercial farm or ranch, then the lot or parcel or portion of 
the lot or parcel is not "generally unsuitable ... A lot or parcel or 
portion of a lot or parcel is presumed to be suitable if it is 
composed predominantly of Class I-IV soils. Just because a lot 
or parcel or portion of a lot or parcel is unsuitable for one farm 
use does not mean it is not suitable for another farm use; or 
Although the parcel is physically separated from adjacent resource lands by 
existing development features, including a shared asphalt driveway to the 
west serving multiple residences and an irrigation canal to the north, 
Planning could not determine that the parcel or portion of the parcel cannot 
reasonably be sold, leased, rented, or otherwise managed as part of a 
commercial farm or ranch operation due to the predominance of Class I-IV 
soils and the agricultural capability classifications of the property.

June 16, 2025 
Josephine County Planning Department 
Grants Pass, Oregon 
Re: Letter of Support - 
Oirling Family Non-Farm Dwelling Application 
To the Josephine County Planning Department 
My name is Emily Bertman, and I am a neighboring property owner at 4751 Lower River 
Rd in Grants Pass. I am writing in strong support of the Dining family's application to 
construct a non-farm dwelling on their property, and to respectfully encourage the 
Department to reconsider its denial of that request. 
I have come to know the Dirling family through our shared road and have spoken at 
length with their son, Ray Jr., about their situation. His parents are aging and are no 
longer able to comfortably navigate the stairs in their current home. The proposed 
dwelling would allow them to remain close to their family, on property their family 
already owns, in a home designed to meet their needs as they get older. Meanwhile, 
Ray Jr. would move from the existing accessory dwelling unit into the parents' current 
home, making full use of the existing structures. This is a thoughtful, family-centered 
plan that causes no waste of resources and no disruption to the surrounding area. 
I understand that the denial was based on a determination that the Dirlings' land is 
fannable. As a neighboring landowner and an active organic tanner myself, I want to 
offer my perspective on that characterization. The field in question lies directly across 
the driveway from my property, and it is my observation, shared, I understand, by at 
least three other tanners who have assessed the land, that it cannot support even basic 
forage crops such as hay. The proposed home site is located near the existing 
residence, and would have minimal footprint relative to the parcel as a whole. In my 
view, designating this ground as productive farmland when experienced local farmers 
have found it unproductive would be a hardship on the Dining family without a 
corresponding agricultural benefit to the community. 
I hope the Department will weigh this appeal with the full picture in mind- a family 
working to care for their elderly parents with dignity, on land that holds little practical 
agricultural value. with a plan that is sensible, modest in scope, and deeply rooted in the 
kind of rural community values we all share here in the Rogue Valley. I wholeheartedly 
support the Dirling family's request and ask that it be approved. 
With appreciation for your consideration, 
Emily Bertman 
Justin Gerlitz

Amanda Courson 
Luis Hernandez support quotes 
To whom it may concern, 
On the morning of July 3, 2026 at 10 AM we, Raymond Dirlingand Amanda Dirling had a conversation with 
Luis S Hernandez, The homeowner of 4803 Lower River Road Grants Pass, OR 97526. Louis Hernandez 
has been farming his 20 acres for the past couple of decades. He is an alfalfa hay and hazelnut farmer. 
He currently has 281 hazelnut trees on his property which is adjacent to the subject properties shared 
driveway. 
He received a letter from the county stating that our approval to build our one-story cottage had been 
denied. He told us because he is 87, He feels he would not like to write a letter however, if his late wife 
was still here, .. she would have written one already." He gave us permission to "please quote him" by 
stating that in his opinion, the EFU land in subject that we have applied to build a house on is not 
farmable land and he would "never farm it". He said "it is too small". He said that "The county should let 
us build our cottage for our elderly parents to age in place in, and he would be happy to see us build." He 
also said that "as a neighbor to the property in question he has no reason to not build but wish's that the 
county would vote to let us build." 
Thank you so much for your time and your consideration, 
Amanda and Raymond Dirling 
To the County Commissioners Office and Whom it May Concern, 
My name is Caleb Hunter. I am a 4th generation farmer here in 
Southern Oregon. My family owns and operates APPLEGATE 
INVESTMENTS Co. located at 6955 HWY 238 Jacksonville, OR 97530. We 
mainly farm alfalfa fields and cattle. We have 240 acres under 
cultivation. My family has been farming our land since the 1950's. I was 
approached by the Dirling family to give my professional opinion on the 
subject property located at ( 36-06-15-CO TL 2800) which is currently 
undeveloped land on Lower River Rd. When researching the property 
location, size and soil I have come to the conclusion that I would NOT 
farm this land. Even if I lived next door. The property size is too small. 
There would be no profit in order to justify the labor and time spent to 
plant any kind of crop. The driveway that the property has an easement 
to use does not allow for my heavy farming equipment to be used 
without harming the road/ driveway that too many other property 
owners use on a daily and also have an easement for use. Lastly, the 
poor soil quality that has been established at this property would not 
satisfy our farming requirements and standards for a favorable and 
successful farm. It is in my professional opinion that the properties best 
use is for generational living. Allowing a small home to be built in order 
for the Dirling's to age gracefully in place and in doing so only using 2% 
of the observed "good soil" is using the property to it's fullest potential 
in both enhancing and preserving value to the property and also 
surrounding properties. 
Thank you for your consideration and your time, 
Caleb Hunter

Technical Opinion Regarding Agricultural 
Non-Viability of The Dirling Revocable 
Living Trust's 2.71 Acre Parcel of 
Undeveloped Land on Lower River Road 
(36-06-15-CO TL 2800) 
This letter is provided to the Dirling Revocable Living Trust to document the expert opinion 
of Richard Vogl, Hydrogeotogist, who holds a Bachelors and Master's degree in Geology 
from California State University, Los Angeles, and is a registered geologist in California, 
Certified Hydrogeologist, and Certified Engineering Geologist, with over 40 years of 
professional experience in the field. This expert opinion is related to the above referenced 
property in Joesphine County, Oregon (Subject Property). 
1. Executive Summary 
The subject property is an undeveloped parcel in southern Oregon with a thin veneer of 
topsoil, ranging from approximately 12 to 36 inches in depth, with an average of 22 inches, 
overlying a dense stream-gravel deposit. These soil conditions were recently assessed and 
documented by numerous potholes conducted across the property by an excavation 
contractor (See attached test pit photos). These physical soil conditions materially restrict 
the effective rooting zone, reduce available water storage, limit nutrient retention, increase 
drought stress, and create severe limitations for sustained crop production. Based on the 
described soil profile, the land should not be characterized as viable cropland without 
extraordinary and economically disproportionate intervention. 
2. Physical Soil Limitations 
Effective soil depth is a controlling factor in crop productivity because plant roots require 
sufficient soil volume for anchorage, water uptake, and nutrient acquisition. Oregon State 
University's forage and soil guidance defines shallow soils as those with 1 Oto 20 inches 
before a restrictive layer and moderately deep soils as those with 20 to 36 inches before 
such a barrier. A restrictive layer may include gravel, rock, dense clay, or other materials 
that impede root development. The reported 12-to-36-inch topsoil depth across the
