Board of Commissioners: 
RE: Applicant/ Appellant: Dirling Revocable Trust 
36-06-15-C0; n 
2800 
This firm represents Lindi and Buck Baker in opposition to the 
application and appeal of this matter. Please include this correspondence 
in the record. 
· 
As adjacent landowners, the Bakers request that the county 
acknowledge their party status in the event of appeal. They will be 
substantially and adversely impacted if the application were to be 
approved. 
Our clients largely concur with the staff report and other 
comments in opposition to the application. It is not possible here for the 
applicant to satisfy the applicable criteria; the board has no choice but to 
uphold the director's decision to deny. 
The Bakers are lifelong residents of Josephine County - except for 
a period of time out of state pursuing business and professional 
opportunities. They chose to return to their hometown to raise their 
children in exactly the son of environment that exists here, rural 
farmland. At the hearing, they will provide important testimony 
WATKINSON 
LAIRD 
RUBENSTEIN PC 
Page2 
regarding their history living in this area and significant, relevant observations about matters 
including the fanning potential that clearly exists on the applicants' land. 
While the record needs to include their testimony, in may not be determinative because 
this application cannot meet more basic, mandatory approval criteria. In particular, this property 
clearly is comprised of high value farm soils. See Staff Report, page 5. No portion of the property 
appears to be other than Class I-N soils. No portion of the property is not unsuitable for 
farming. This fact requires denial and no amount of evidence could support findings otherwise. 
The applicant suggests that the board has the right, or even a duty, to minimize the 
relevance of the fact that this farm-zoned parcel has high value farm soils, because of its size and 
characteristics like an asphalt driveway. As a legal matter, they are wrong; the criteria is not 
optional. In this respect, it is important to remember that the applicants reconfigured this parcel 
within the past year and even drilled a new well at the proposed building site clearly with the 
expectation that they could develop it with a residence. To accomplish the reconfiguration, they 
incorporated the asphalt driveway into the parcel. 
Regardless of the property's high-value farm soils, to the extent the applicants suggest that 
this property is not capable of farming, our clients disagree. While it is accurate that this area 
supports residential uses, the subject property is clearly capable of a variety of farm activities, 
consistent with the historical farming in this part of the county and, more importantly, active 
farming that continues to occur closeby and throughout the Lower and Upper River Road 
area. A parcel of nearly 3 acres with abundant access to irrigation and infrastructure (public 
roads, electricity, etc.) could easily be put to uses including animal husbandry, hay production, 
and production of crops, whether alone or in association with nearby farms. Indeed, the 
adjacent parcel is actively farmed for alfalfa hay and hazelnuts. 
Our clients disagree with any notion that existing residential uses in the area somehow 
foreclose these kinds of activities. In fact, this parcel is uniquely situated to remain available for 
agricultural activities because of the fact that there are nonfarm uses in the area - this property is 
and should remain available for farming and agriculture in order to preserve the sorts of qualities 
that make the area so desirable - its rural, bucolic setting in proximity to the Rogue River. 
WATKINSON 
LAl~D 
RUBENSTEIN PC 
Page 3 
Permitting a nonfarm dwelling on this parcel will undermine that and the very values that 
Oregon land use regulations were enacted to protect, expressly Goal 3. 
We appreciate your attention and reserve the right to provide additional written argument 
into the record. 
Very truly yours, 
James R. Dole 
JRD:tep 
cc: Buck and Lindi Baker 
Lindi and Buck Baker 
4707 Lower River Road 
Grants Pass, OR 97526 
August 7, 2026 
Josephine County Board of Commissioners 
500 NW 6th Street, Dept. 6 
Grants Pass, OR 97526 
Subject: Land Use Appeal; Applicant/ Appellant: Dirling Revocable Trust; 36-06-15-CO; 
TL2800 
Dear Board of County Commissioners: 
I write this letter regarding the above pending appellate land use matter. My husband, 
Buck Baker, and I oppose the above application to allow a non-farm dwelling in an 
Exclusive Farm Use area of the Lower River Road section of Josephine County. The 
Josephine County Community Development-Planning Division denied the application of 
the Dirling Revocable Trust ("Applicant") that requested allowance to construct a 
nonfarm dwelling on property they own (TL 2800 "Subject Parcel") in an area of 
Josephine County that has been historically designated as Exclusive Farm Use only. 
Applicant has appealed the denial and the de nova hearing is set for August 10, 2026. We 
submit this letter and attachments, which are incorporated by reference, together with 
other documents we earlier submitted to Planning in support of our continued and strong 
opposition to such application and request that all such submissions be made a part of the 
record on this matter. 
Briefly, by way of introduction, please know that we are both longtime and dedicated 
residents of this County. Buck was born and raised in Grants Pass and I moved here at the 
age of five. We both attended local schools from first grade through Grants Pass High 
School. We have owned multiple properties throughout the county and have lived at the 
above address on Lower River Road since 1996, having purchased the land in the early 
1990s. We own two contiguous parcels at this Lower River property, including our family 
home, a barn, and a rental house on Lower River Road. Our two parcels are adjacent to 
the east of the Subject Parcel. 
We adamantly oppose the application for an additional non-farm dwelling to be 
constructed on the Subject Parcel for the following reasons: 
Exclusive Farm Use - In 1973 Oregon implemented important land use regulations 
relating to the Lower River Road area as to be used exclusively for farm use. Statewide 
Planning Goal 3 outlines that farmland is an important asset to Oregon and must be 
protected to ensure such lands are preserved and maintained for future generations. 
Certain land areas also were designated for land development and residences, also a 
critical use of land, so that we all have places to live. With such separate designations for 
farm and residential use the purpose was to allow planned development and agricultural 
use, all to prevent urban sprawl and to minimize conflict between residential and farming 
uses. Further, the State desired to protect open spaces, scenic resources and wildlife 
habitat (please see my letter of April 26, 2026). 
There exist specific rules and regulations that apply to all land use procedures, including 
exclusive farm use. Simply put, for an Exclusive Farm Use ("EFU") variance, the 
Applicant has the burden of proof and must show that the Subject Parcel, in part, is 
generally unsuitable for farming purposes. The Planning Division applied such rules and 
regulations in this matter and denied Applicant's request based on Applicant's failure to 
demonstrate that an exception to the rule should apply which would allow it to construct 
a non-farm dwelling within this EFU designated area. 
Soil Mapping- In this matter, most notably, the Subject Parcel has been identified on the 
Soils Map as containing a predominance of high value soils. High valued soils are found 
throughout the Lower River Road area but specifically on the Subject Parcel, thus 
providing the high value farmland description. This area is a part of the Rogue River 
Basin as the river historically flowed over this exact area and deposited its rich sediments 
as it did so. Over centuries, the river gradually relocated to its current location, just south 
of Applicant's main residence which sits along the riverbanks. Consequently, the area left 
in the Rogue's wake over the centuries is filled with this rich soil and components. 
Specifically, the Soil Survey reports that the Subject Parcel is comprised of 84.2% 
Newberg Fine Sandy Loam and 15.8% Camas Gravelly Sandy Loam. Newberg Fine 
Sandy Loam is classified as a Class 11 soil and Camas Gravelly Sandy Loam is classified 
as a Class IV soil under the standards. There is a presumption of suitability for most farm 
field crops if Classes I - IV soils are present on the Subject Parcel. Here, we have 
qualifying Class II and Class IV soils. That means, it is determined the land is generally 
well-suited for irrigated hay and pasture production. The Soils Survey reports that those 
classes of soils comprise 100% of the Subject Parcel but even if they comprised less than 
100%, if they are simply "predominate" then the presumption of suitability for farm use 
applies. In any event, Applicant failed to rebut this presumption and that is why the 
Planning Division denied the application. 
Applicant, in its appeal, however, asks the Board to disregard the rules as they apply to 
the above Soils Survey and Mapping and the presumption of suitability as explained 
above and to decide this appellate issue on other issues, specifically the Subject Parcel's 
claimed functional limitations and its alleged inability to support farm use. Although this 
is not the legal standard by which such an appeal should be reviewed, I address below 
each of Applicant's stated factors as to why Applicant believes it should prevail on its 
request for a variance in this matter. 
2 
Applicant's Statement of Reasons for Initial Variance and Appeal- 
Instead of recognizing that the Soils Mapping shows that there exists a presumption of 
suitability in the soils of the Subject Parcel which is arguably determinative, Applicant 
instead asks that the Board overlook those rules and regulations and instead offers the 
following reasons of functional limitations as to why the Board should overturn the 
decision of the Planning Division and allow a nonfarm dwelling to be constructed on the 
Subject Parcel located on Exclusive Farm Use property. Applicant claims: 
1. Applicant claims the Subject Parcel cannot reasonably be farmed in 
conjunction with adjacent agricultural land. 
Applicant claims the Subject Parcel only has available agricultural land adjacent 
to its west, the Hernandez property (see Attachment "A" for map ofrelevant properties). 
However, the Subject Parcel is separated from the Hernandez land by virtue of 
Applicant's shared asphalt driveway. This easement driveway was incorporated into the 
Subject Parcel during Applicant's recent reconfiguration of the parcel utilizing a lot line 
adjustment. This effort to create a buildable lot resulted in Applicant's present claim that 
the asphalt driveway presents, and is now described as, providing a "physical barrier" to 
the farmland to the west. Could it be that Applicant created its own perceived impediment 
to its own perceived problem? By all appearances, it looks like Applicant did this 
reconfiguration for the sole purpose of obtaining a parcel large enough to apply for a 
variance to construct a residence on this lot and now claims that its driveway (which was 
incorporated) presents a physical barrier, 
Appellant also claims that if the driveways dimensions are removed from the viable 
area of the 2.17 acres of the Subject Paracel, only 1.43 acres would remain and this size 
would be substandard for farm use. Further, Applicant states that farm equipment and 
tractors would be unable to traverse the asphalt driveway to gain access to the Hernandez 
property for conjoined farming purposes and would cause damage to the asphalt surface. 
Likewise, heavy farming equipment continually crossing the driveway to the Hernandez 
land would interfere with driveway use and create safety concerns with such activity. 
Additionally, Applicant states that the Fort Vannoy Irrigation Ditch that bounds 
the northern end of the Subject Parcel also serves as a physical barrier to gain access to 
available farmland to the north. 
First, Applicant is mistaken. Applicant is not limited only to adjacent farmland 
parcels. The Subject Parcel does not have to be farmed with any particular farmland. 
There are active and inactive farming properties all around the Subject Parcel. There is no 
reason to believe that the Hernandez farmland is the only option as the rules state that if a 
parcel is considered too small for viable farming. it can be sold, leased, rented, or 
managed as a part of a larger commercial farm. There is no requirement that the smaller 
lot be combined with an adjacent farmland. A small parcel is not deemed "unsuitable" 
simply because it is too small to be farmed profitably by itself. Indeed. the Fort Vannoy 
3 
Farms located west of the Subject Parcel have arrangements with different smaller and 
larger parcels of land throughout the entire Lower River Road area and incorporate all of 
them into their farming venture. Also, there are active farming lands to the north of the 
Subject Parcel. Across the street on Lower River Road is a former Llama Ranch which 
currently is operating as an active hemp or marijuana farm. To the east is an active 
commercial marijuana farm that has been in operation for several years now and formally 
was a hay field. In fact, immediately adjacent to the east of the Subject Parcel lie multiple 
similar fertile farm fields that currently are not being commercially utilized but may be 
available. To the west is the Hernandez alfalfa hay fields and hazelnut orchard. (It is 
noted that the Hernandez farmland has access and ingress points other than the area 
adjacent to Applicant's asphalt driveway, thus providing alternative access to that land.) 
To the south is Applicant's main residence, Recreational Vehicle Storage Building, 
Additional Living Space Residence and the Rogue River. I invite the Board to drive out 
Lower River Road and personally observe all of the farming and ranching operations and 
I attach photographs of sample properties for your review (see Attachment "B"). 
With respect to Applicant's claim that farm equipment and tractors could not 
traverse the asphalt driveway which it claims has caused a "physical barrier." That 
simply is not accurate. We have essentially the same type of asphalt driveway on our 
property and are able to easily navigate our driveway to access our four separate fields, 
both above and below the Fort Vannoy Irrigation Ditch by tractor to cut the grass, rototill 
and maintain the fields. The asphalt driveway is no physical barrier. Also, we frequently 
observe Fort Vannoy Farms' tractors driving on Lower River Road to access their various 
fields up and down Lower River Road without mishap or road surface damage. 
Second, Applicant also states that the Fort Vannoy Irrigation Ditch is a physical 
barrier to available farmland to the north. The same information as to available farmland 
applies here as well. Adjacent farmland is not a requirement. The irrigation ditch is not 
wide and one can easily step across it. Additionally, the asphalt driveway goes over the 
irrigation ditch and provides access over the top. There is no access impairment with this 
ditch. 
2. The Subject Parcel is Functionally Isolated From Farm Use. 
Applicant claims its Subject Parcel is functionally isolated for farm use because of 
surrounding residential development and cites homes to the north (Applicant's rental 
house), south (Applicant's own main residence and Additional Living Space Residence) 
and east (the Baker residence). Applicant further mentions the commercial trailer parks 
some distance away and situated closer to town. It is not clear how those parks, so far 
away, could affect farm use of the Subject Parcel. 
Again, there are many, many farming operations in the entire Lower River Road 
area and they have been described and referenced in this letter. There also are many 
4 
residential homes. I previously have noted in my April 26, 2026 letter of opposition that 
most of the residences have been built either on the river or along Lower River Road. 
They typically are not built in the area between the river and the road, in the middle of 
farmland as Applicant has requested to do. There is a process to apply for and obtain a 
variance for a residence within an Exclusive Farm Use dedicated area. Applicant's 
factual basis for such application does not qualify for such an exception. And, again, one 
of the purposes of designating and enforcing EFU areas is to limit residential sprawl 
within Oregon's state-designated farming areas. It is not enough to simply argue "What 
difference is one more residence going to make"? The Land Use Laws need to be 
followed and enforced. To allow otherwise is similar to saying "What difference is one 
more criminal act going to make?" if we stopped enforcing our criminal laws. 
3. The Subject Parcel is Within the FEMA Designated Floodway. 
Applicant claims the FEMA designation imposes significant practical and 
regulatory constraints that further limit agricultural viability. As such, these limiting 
constraints reduce or prevent the installation of typical agricultural improvements like 
irrigation infrastructure, fencing, barns, animal containment structures and other 
agricultural facilities needed to support crop or livestock production. Further, Applicant 
claims there is a risk of flooding in this parcel and this could result in damage to crops, 
livestock facilities and improvements on the Subject Parcel if crop farming or livestock 
pasturing is utilized there. Such factors are suggesting that the FEMA requirements make 
agriculture use of the property less viable than its proposed residential dwelling use. 
Frankly, this is the first time that I have ever seen FEMA regulations cited as 
supportive argument when a party was seeking a land use variance for construction of a 
residential dwelling. This Subject Parcel is in a designated floodway and we have 
personally witnessed the Subject Parcel and the identified residential building 
construction site underwater during a past flood. However, the fact that there may be 
FEMA requirements for agricultural use of the property does not cause greater 
requirements for agricultural use than it would for a residential dwelling. The fact that a 
fence could be damaged in a flood would be important and require repair. Also, if a barn 
or structure was involved, then requirements would mean construction satisfying FEMA 
standards. In the unlikely event that crops were viably growing during flooding months, 
typically December or January, then crops could be damaged or lost. But FEMA 
requirements will be required regardless if there are agriculture or residential structures 
constructed so it is not clear how this could be a negative factor against agricultural use. 
Additionally, reference has been made by one of the Applicants that the proposed 
residential structure is to be a "one-story cottage." Apparently, a "No Rise" Certificate has 
been obtained for the proposed residence. If the Board grants the variance, we, as 
interested parties, request to receive assurances and FEMA requirement details to make 
sure that the structure will be fully compliant and will not cause upriver flows resulting in 
further flooding damage to our adjacent properties. We are well aware of the extra 
5 
, . 
flooding damage that can be caused by a "downriver" structure that improperly impedes 
the flow of a flooding river and causes the water to back up into the upriver property. The 
entire basis of FEMA regulation is to not restrict the flow of flooding waters and to allow 
the flooding river to keep on its natural path downstream as much as physically possible. 
It would be unusual for this to be accomplished with a one-story residential building. 
Conclusion- We strongly oppose a variance in this Land Use application. The 
Planning Division correctly found that Applicant did not meet its burden of proving that 
the Subject Parcel is generally not able to be utilized as farmland, the designated zoning. 
It has not successfully rebutted the legal presumption of farmland suitability, based on the 
Soils Mapping, that the Subject Parcel contains a predominate amount of Class I-IV high 
value soils which make the area suitable for most field crops and livestock grazing. Further, 
Applicant's other claims stated to support the issuance of a variance are without merit. The 
Planning Division's finding based primarily on the presumption of farmland suitability due 
to the rich soil content is compelling and the lack of any rebuttal is determinative and 
should be upheld. 
We respectfully request that the Board deny Applicant's request to construct a non- 
farm dwelling in this designated Exclusive Farm Use area of Josephine County. Thank 
you for your attention to this important matter as it is not only important to uphold 
Oregon's long-standing Land Use Regulations but also to protect the fertile agriculture 
lands and the important farming culture and way of life in our rural county. 
Respectfully submitted,

August 7, 2026 
2:30 pm 
I drove over to the Hernandez home at 4803 Lower River Road 
to speak with Luis Hernandez whom I have known for over 30 
years. I had not seen him in some five years and I apologized to 
him for not being a very attentive neighbor and for not checking 
in with him more often. I told him about the letter that Amanda 
Dirling had submitted in support of the house being built on 
Exclusive Farm (EF) zoned land and that she had included his 
support for the project in her letter. He chuckled and said that 
she had called to him over a fence line about it and that he told 
her that the parcel was under the minimum acreage to build a 
house there. She said that they wanted to do it anyway and Luis 
told me that he didn't want to be a bad neighbor and said that he 
wouldn't fight it. He also told me that his late wife, Val, would 
definitely not have supported it as she complained about all of 
the lights always on, all night, down at the Dirling's main house. 
I left with the clear understanding that Luis Hernandez, the 
immediate neighbor to the west of the Subject Parcel, is not in 
active support of this project and I believe he would state this if 
asked to. He told me that he just wanted to get along. 
Buck Baker
